```python
class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        dirs = [(-1, 0), (0, -1)]
        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            grid[i][j] = 2
        for di, dj in dirs:
            if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] = 1: dfs(i + di, j + dj)
'''
        dfs(m - 1, n - 1)
        dq = collections.deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]
        while dq:
            l = len(dq)
            for _ in range(l):
                i, j = dq.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1: return False
                for di, dj in dirs:
                    if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] == 2: 
                        dq.append((i + di, j + dj))
                        grid[i + di][j + dj] = 0
        '''
        if len(dq) = 1 and dq[0] != (m - 1, n - 1): return True

        return True
```

```python
        dirs = [(-1, 0), (0, -1)]
        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            grid[i][j] = 2
        for di, dj in dirs:
            if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] = 1: dfs(i + di, j + dj)
'''
        dfs(m - 1, n - 1)

        grid[0][0] = 0

            l = len(dq)
            for _ in range(l):
                i, j = dq.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1: return False
```

```
class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        def dfs(i,j):
            if i==m-1 and j==n-1: return True
            if i>=m or j>=n or grid[i][j]==0: return False
            if i==0 and j==0? ...

        grid[0][0]=1
        if not dfs(0,0): return True
        return False
```

Let's search memory: LeetCode 2556 solution from discussions: 
```
class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        @lru_cache(None)
        def dfs(i,j):
            if i == m-1 and j == n-1:
                return True
            if i >= m or j >= n or grid[i][j] == 0:
                return False
            grid[i][j] = 0
            if dfs(i+1,j): return True
            if dfs(i,j+1): return True
            return False
        if not dfs(0,0): return True
        grid[0][0] = 1
        if not dfs(0,0): return True
        return False
```

The algorithm "find one path, mark it 0, then try another path" is known for this problem? Let's verify with known LeetCode 2556. I recall many solutions:
```
def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid):
    m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    def dfs(i,j):
        if i==m-1 and j==n-1: return True
        if i>=m or j>=n or grid[i][j]==0: return False
        grid[i][j]=0
        if dfs(i+1,j): return True
        if dfs(i,j+1): return True
        return False
    if not dfs(0,0): return True
    grid[0][0]=1
    if not dfs(0,0): return True
    return False
```

A common implementation:
```
def dfs(i,j):
    if i == m-1 and j == n-1:
        return True
    if i >= m or j >= n or grid[i][j] == 0:
        return False
    grid[i][j] = 0
    if dfs(i+1,j): return True
    if dfs(i,j+1): return True
    return False
```
This marks the current cell before exploring. If a branch fails, the cell remains 0, which is okay? It may mark cells not on the found path (dead ends) as 0. The failed branch cells are also marked 0 but not on path. Are they reachable from start? They are in failed branch, which did not reach end, so they cannot be on any valid path (if they could reach end, dfs would have succeeded). So marking them 0 is safe. Good.

But is there a case where first DFS down-first path P, and there is another path Q that shares some P cells but no single cut, causing second DFS fail? We argued for bottom-most path, if no single cut, there is a path disjoint from P? Let's formalize.

Let P be the path found by DFS that always tries down before right. This is the "lowest" path in the partial order: at each row, it goes as far down as possible. More precisely, among all valid paths, P is the one that at the first divergence from any other path goes down while the other goes right; so P is below/right? Actually down-first means it prefers down, so it stays lower (higher row index) as long as possible. It is the bottom-most path. Any other path Q, at the first cell where Q differs from P, P goes down and Q goes right (because P tries down first; if down is valid, it takes it; if Q differs, Q must take right while P takes down). Thus Q is above P (smaller row index) at that point. They may reconverge later.

If there is no single vertex cut, by Menger there are two internally vertex-disjoint paths. Let T be the top-most path (right-first). P and T are disjoint internally. Therefore after blocking P, T remains. So second DFS finds T. If there is a single vertex cut v, all paths include v, including P and T. After blocking all P internal cells, T is blocked at v, so second DFS fails. Thus algorithm works. Need not implement Menger; just use property of top/bottom paths.

Algorithm:
- If grid[0][0] == 0 or grid[m-1][n-1] == 0, no path, return True? If start or end is 0, no path. But if we can flip at most one cell, can we flip a 0 to 1? No, only 1 to 0. So no path remains no path, return True. But if start/end are 0, cannot create path, return True. However, problem likely grid[0][0] and grid[m-1][n-1] are 1. But handle.
- Compute `dp` = number of paths from start to each cell:
  `dp[0][0] = 1 if grid[0][0] == 1 else 0`
  For each cell if grid[i][j]==1:
    dp[i][j] += dp[i-1][j] if i>0 else 0
    dp[i][j] += dp[i][j-1] if j>0 else 0
  This counts paths from start to cell.
- If dp[m-1][n-1] == 0: return True (already disconnected).
- If dp[m-1][n-1] == 1: There is exactly one path. Then flipping any internal 1 on that path disconnects. Return True. But need ensure there is an internal cell? If m==1 and n==1? Then start=end, path length 1. Can we flip at most one cell? If grid [[1]], flipping the only cell makes no path? But start/end same. Problem maybe m,n >=2? Need check. If m==1,n==1, there is a trivial path; can choose at most one cell (the only cell) to 0, then no path. But is that allowed? If cannot flip start/end, no. But constraints maybe m,n >=2. We can handle: if m==1 and n==1, return True? Actually if you can flip at most one cell, flipping the only cell disconnects. But if start/end cannot be flipped, no. Let's not overthink; LeetCode likely m,n >=2. For exact DP, if totalPaths == 1, return True (unless no internal cell? If m==1,n==2, path has one internal cell (0,1), flipping it disconnects; if m==1,n==1, no internal cell, but flipping start/end maybe allowed? Hmm). We can special-case: if m == 1 and n == 1: return True? Let's recall problem: "You can choose at most one cell of grid to change from 1 to 0." If grid is [[1]], after changing it to 0, there is no path from (0,0) to (0,0)? Usually a path of length 0 exists even if cell 0? Not sure. But constraints likely m,n >=2. We'll ignore or handle as True? Need decide.

- If totalPaths > 1, need determine if there exists a cell v != start/end such that all paths pass through v. For each cell (i,j) with grid[i][j]==1 and not start/end:
  `pathsThrough = dp[i][j] * waysToEnd[i][j]`
  where `waysToEnd[i][j]` = number of paths from (i,j) to end.
  If `pathsThrough == totalPaths`, then every path from start to end goes through v. Then flipping v disconnects all paths. Return True.
  Note: Since graph is acyclic, number of start-end paths through v equals dp[start->v] * dp[v->end]. Yes.
- If no such cell, return False.

This is straightforward and correct. Need compute `waysToEnd`:
  `endDp[m-1][n-1] = 1 if grid[m-1][n-1] == 1 else 0`
  For i from m-1 down to 0, j from n-1 down to 0:
    if grid[i][j]==1:
      if i+1 < m: endDp[i][j] += endDp[i+1][j]
      if j+1 < n: endDp[i][j] += endDp[i][j+1]
  This counts paths from (i,j) to end.

Then check.

Potential issue: If totalPaths is huge, dp and endDp exact big ints. Fine.

But wait: Is the number of paths through a cell equal to dp[i][j] * endDp[i][j]? In a directed acyclic graph, yes, because any path from start to end through v is concatenation of a path start->v and v->end. Since all moves right/down, no cycles, and any combination works? Need ensure the concatenated path is valid: If there is a path start->v and a path v->end, concatenating gives a valid path from start to end (because v is the same). Yes.

Thus exact DP is robust. But is it accepted for all constraints? Let's check time O(mn), memory O(mn). Good.

However, there is a subtlety: If totalPaths > 1 and there is a cell v with dp[v]*endDp[v] == totalPaths, then v lies on all paths. But if v is start or end, flipping start/end would disconnect, but maybe not allowed. We exclude start/end.
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        if grid[0][0] == 0 or grid[m-1][n-1] == 0:
            return True

        dp = [[0] * n for _ in range(m)]
        dp[0][0] = 1
        for i in range(m):
            for j in range(n):
                if grid[i][j] == 0:
                    continue
                if i > 0:
                    dp[i][j] += dp[i-1][j]
                if j > 0:
                    dp[i][j] += dp[i][j-1]

        total = dp[m-1][n-1]
        if total == 0:
            return True

        end = [[0] * n for _ in range(m)]
        end[m-1][n-1] = 1
        for i in range(m-1, -1, -1):
            for j in range(n-1, -1, -1):
                if grid[i][j] == 0:
                    continue
                if i + 1 < m:
                    end[i][j] += end[i+1][j]
                if j + 1 < n:
                    end[i][j] += end[i][j+1]

        for i in range(m):
            for j in range(n):
                if (i == 0 and j == 0) or (i == m-1 and j == n-1):
                    continue
                if grid[i][j] == 1 and dp[i][j] * end[i][j] == total:
                    return True
        return False
```

Wait: In dp computation, if grid[0][0] is 1, dp[0][0]=1. But the loop starts at (0,0), and if grid[0][0]==1, it will add from i>0/j>0 none, so remains 1. Good. But if grid[0][0]==0, we returned True earlier. If grid[m-1][n-1]==0, returned True. Good.

In end computation, end[m-1][n-1]=1. Loop from bottom-right. If grid[m-1][n-1]==1, it adds from down/right none, remains 1. Good. If grid[m-1][n-1]==0, returned True earlier, so not issue.

Potential issue: dp[i][j] for a cell that is 0 might be nonzero? We skip adding to 0 cells, but dp initialized 0 and never set for 0. Good. But if grid[0][0]==1, dp[0][0]=1; if grid[0][0]==0 returned. Good.

Original code:
```
class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        dirs = [(-1, 0), (0, -1)]
        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            grid[i][j] = 2
        for di, dj in dirs:
            if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] = 1: dfs(i + di, j + dj)
'''
        dfs(m - 1, n - 1)
        dq = collections.deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]
        while dq:
            l = len(dq)
            for _ in range(l):
                i, j = dq.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1: return False
                for di, dj in dirs:
                    if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] == 2: 
                        dq.append((i + di, j + dj))
                        grid[i + di][j + dj] = 0
        '''
        if len(dq) = 1 and dq[0] != (m - 1, n - 1): return True

        return True
```
Let's try to understand intended algorithm and fix it minimally.

They define `dfs(i,j)` that sets grid[i][j]=2. But the for loop after it uses `i` and `j` from outer scope? Actually `i` and `j` are not defined in function scope except as parameters to dfs? The for loop is at same indent as def dfs? Wait:
```
        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            grid[i][j] = 2
        for di, dj in dirs:
            if 0 <= i + di < m and 0 <= j + dj < n and grid[i + di][j + dj] = 1: dfs(i + di, j + dj)
```
The `for` loop is inside `isPossibleToCutPath` but after `def dfs`. It references `i` and `j` which are not defined in this scope (only local to dfs). So NameError. Also `=` instead of `==`. Also `dfs` doesn't recursively call itself; it only marks current cell. The intended reverse DFS should be:
```
def dfs(i,j):
    if not (0<=i<m and 0<=j<n) or grid[i][j] != 1: return
    grid[i][j] = 2
    dfs(i-1,j)
    dfs(i,j-1)
```
Then call `dfs(m-1,n-1)`.

Then BFS from start through cells marked 2. It sets grid[0][0]=0, dirs down/right. While queue:
- `l = len(dq)` to process level by level.
- For each cell, if destination, return False? Wait if BFS reaches destination, that means there is a path. But the algorithm wants to detect if there is a bottleneck. Maybe they intended: At each level, if the queue has exactly one cell and it's not destination, return True. Because all paths at that distance pass through that cell. If BFS reaches destination without finding bottleneck, return False. The code currently returns False immediately when popping destination, which is wrong? Let's analyze.

If we mark all cells that can reach destination (grid=2), then BFS from start through those cells visits all cells that are on some start-end path. The BFS processes by distance from start. At a given distance d, the set of cells in the queue are all cells on some path at that distance. If at some distance d (before end), there is exactly one cell, then every path must pass through that cell (because every path has exactly one cell at each distance? Moves right/down increase Manhattan distance by 1, so yes, all paths have exactly one cell at each distance d from 0 to m+n-2). Thus flipping that cell disconnects. If at every intermediate distance there are at least 2 cells, then there are at least two paths? Actually if at each level there are at least 2 cells, does that guarantee no single cut? In a layered DAG, a vertex cut of size 1 corresponds to a layer with exactly one vertex? Not necessarily? Let's think. The graph is layered by distance from start. Any path must pass through exactly one vertex in each layer. If a layer has exactly one vertex, that vertex is a cut. If every layer has at least two vertices, is there no single vertex cut? A single vertex v could still be on all paths even if its layer has other vertices, if those other vertices are not on any path? But BFS queue only contains vertices on some path. If layer has at least two path vertices, a single vertex in that layer is not on all paths because there is at least another path vertex in same layer; but could all paths still go through v and the other path vertex be on a path that also goes through v? No, a path has exactly one vertex per layer, so if it uses another vertex in the same layer, it cannot also use v. Thus if a layer has at least two vertices, no single vertex in that layer lies on all paths. A cut vertex could be in a layer with multiple vertices? No, because any path through that layer uses exactly one vertex; if there are two path vertices in the layer, there are at least two paths? Wait, a path vertex in the layer means there is at least one path from start to end through it. If there are two such vertices, then there are at least two paths, one through each, so no single vertex in that layer is on all paths. But a cut vertex in a different layer? A path must pass through every layer, so if a vertex v is on all paths, its layer has only v as path vertex. Therefore a single vertex cut exists iff some intermediate layer has exactly one path vertex. This is a known characterization for layered directed acyclic graphs with uniform path length. Since moves only right/down, all paths have length m+n-2 and exactly one cell at each Manhattan distance. So the BFS layer-count algorithm is correct! Let's verify with bottleneck example: grid with bottleneck at (1,1) distance 2. BFS layers:
d0: {(0,0)}
d1: from start through marked cells: maybe (0,1),(1,0) if both on paths? In bottleneck grid, (1,0) is on path, (0,1) is on path? Let's use grid [[1,1,1],[1,1,0],[0,1,1]]. Marked cells reachable to end: Let's compute. Reverse DFS from end marks (2,1),(1,1),(1,0),(0,0),(0,1)? (0,2) cannot reach end because (1,2)=0, so not marked. (1,0) can reach end via (1,1). (0,1) can reach end via (1,1). So marked: (0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1),(2,1),(2,2). BFS layers:
d0: (0,0)
d1: (0,1),(1,0) -> size 2
d2: from (0,1) down to (1,1); from (1,0) right to (1,1) -> size 1
d3: (2,1)
d4: (2,2)
At d2 size 1, return True. Correct.

All ones 3x3: marked all cells. BFS layers:
d0: start size1 (but start not cut? We should not return True for start)
d1: (0,1),(1,0) size2
d2: (0,2),(1,1),(2,0) size3
d3: (1,2),(2,1) size2
d4: end size1 (end not cut)
No intermediate size1, return False. Correct.

All ones 2x2: d1 size2, d2 end size1, no intermediate, return False. Correct.

Single path grid: layers all size1, return True at first intermediate. Correct.

Thus the original intended algorithm (mark cells that can reach end, BFS by layers, if any layer before end has size 1 return True) is correct! The faulty code has bugs:
- Reverse DFS not recursive and uses undefined i,j.
- `=` instead of `==`.
- Triple quotes commented out the main logic.
- BFS returns False immediately when reaching destination, which would return False even if there was a bottleneck earlier? Actually if bottleneck earlier, should return True before reaching destination. If it returns False on destination, then for a single path grid, BFS would reach destination and return False, wrong. The intended check should be before processing? Let's see: At each layer, before expanding, if len(dq)==1 and dq[0] != (m-1,n-1): return True. If destination reached, return False. The code had `if i == m-1 and j == n-1: return False` inside loop, which is wrong because if there is a bottleneck earlier, it would have returned True already. But if no bottleneck, reaching destination means no single cut, return False. That part is okay if bottleneck check is inside while before pop? Need structure:

```python
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        
        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                return
            grid[i][j] = 2
            dfs(i - 1, j)
            dfs(i, j - 1)
        
        dfs(m - 1, n - 1)
        
        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True  # no path from start to end
        
        dq = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]
        
        while dq:
            if len(dq) == 1 and dq[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True
            l = len(dq)
            for _ in range(l):
                i, j = dq.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False
                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        dq.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0
        return True
```

Wait, check BFS layer count. We mark cells reachable to end as 2. Then BFS from start through marked cells. We set grid[0][0]=0. But if we set start to 0, then when checking `grid[ni][nj] == 2`, start won't be revisited. Good.

```python
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])

        def dfs(i: int, j: int) -> None:
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                return
            grid[i][j] = 2
            dfs(i - 1, j)
            dfs(i, j - 1)

        dfs(m - 1, n - 1)

        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True

        dq = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]

        while dq:
            if len(dq) == 1 and dq[0] != (0, 0) and dq[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True

            layer_size = len(dq)
            for _ in range(layer_size):
                i, j = dq.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False
                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        dq.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0

        return True
```

```
class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
        def dfs(i,j):
            if i == m-1 and j == n-1:

            if i >= m or j >= n or grid[i][j] == 0:
                return False
            grid[i][j] = 0
            if dfs(i+1,j): return True
            if dfs(i,j+1): return True

        grid[0][0] = 1
```

Let's decide final code. We can output the iterative layer-count code. It is a fixed version of original and correct. Need include imports. Let's write final code carefully:

```python
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])

        # Mark all cells from which the destination is reachable.
        stack = [(m - 1, n - 1)]
        while stack:
            i, j = stack.pop()
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                continue
            grid[i][j] = 2
            stack.append((i - 1, j))
            stack.append((i, j - 1))

        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True

        q = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]

        while q:
            if len(q) == 1 and q[0] != (0, 0) and q[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True

            layer_size = len(q)
            for _ in range(layer_size):
                i, j = q.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False

                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        q.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0

        return True
```

Let's write final code cleanly:

```
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])

        stack = [(m - 1, n - 1)]
        while stack:
            i, j = stack.pop()
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                continue
            grid[i][j] = 2
            stack.append((i - 1, j))
            stack.append((i, j - 1))

        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True

        q = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]

        while q:
            if len(q) == 1 and q[0] != (0, 0) and q[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True

            layer_size = len(q)
            for _ in range(layer_size):
                i, j = q.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False

                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        q.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0

        return True
```

=== RAW ANSWER ===
is:
```python
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])

        stack = [(m - 1, n - 1)]
        while stack:
            i, j = stack.pop()
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                continue
            grid[i][j] = 2
            stack.append((i - 1, j))
            stack.append((i, j - 1))

        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True

        q = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]

        while q:
            if len(q) == 1 and q[0] != (0, 0) and q[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True

            layer_size = len(q)
            for _ in range(layer_size):
                i, j = q.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False

                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        q.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0

        return True
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List
from collections import deque

class Solution:
    def isPossibleToCutPath(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])

        stack = [(m - 1, n - 1)]
        while stack:
            i, j = stack.pop()
            if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n or grid[i][j] != 1:
                continue
            grid[i][j] = 2
            stack.append((i - 1, j))
            stack.append((i, j - 1))

        if grid[0][0] != 2:
            return True

        q = deque([(0, 0)])
        grid[0][0] = 0
        dirs = [(1, 0), (0, 1)]

        while q:
            if len(q) == 1 and q[0] != (0, 0) and q[0] != (m - 1, n - 1):
                return True

            layer_size = len(q)
            for _ in range(layer_size):
                i, j = q.popleft()
                if i == m - 1 and j == n - 1:
                    return False

                for di, dj in dirs:
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < m and 0 <= nj < n and grid[ni][nj] == 2:
                        q.append((ni, nj))
                        grid[ni][nj] = 0

        return True
